A Superintegrable Quantum Field Theory
Authors: Marine De Clerck, Oleg Evnin
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
Gérard and Grellier proposed, under the name of the cubic Szegő equation, a remarkable classical field theory on a circle with a quartic Hamiltonian. The Lax integrability structure that emerges from their definition is so constraining that it allows for writing down an explicit general solution for prescribed initial data, and at the same time, the dynamics is highly nontrivial and involves turbulent energy transfer to arbitrarily short wavelengths. The quantum version of the same Hamiltonian is even more striking: not only the Hamiltonian itself, but also its associated conserved hierarchies display purely integer spectra, indicating a structure beyond ordinary quantum integrability. Here, we initiate a systematic study of this quantum system by presenting a mixture of analytic results and empirical observations on the structure of its eigenvalues and eigenvectors, conservation laws, ladder operators, etc.

Introduction

Our main concern here will be with the quantum Hamiltonian1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H\equiv \frac{1}{2}\sum _{n+m=k+l}^{n,m,k,l\ge 0} a^\dagger _n a^\dagger _m a_k a_l, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H≡12∑n+m=k+ln,m,k,l≥0an†am†akal,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^\dagger _n$$\end{document}an† and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_n$$\end{document}an with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 0$$\end{document}n≥0 are a set of usual bosonic creation-annihilation operators satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[a_n, a_m^\dagger ]=\delta _{mn}$$\end{document}[an,am†]=δmn. The structure is that of a typical particle-number-conserving (nonrelativistic) quantum field theory with quartic interactions, as one may commonly see in theoretical condensed matter physics, yet the couplings between the modes are chosen in such a way as to impart the system a rich and elaborate integrability structure. Our goal is to investigate this structure.

The classical version of (1.1) is the Hamiltonian1.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{cl}\equiv \frac{1}{2}\sum _{n+m=k+l}^{n,m,k,l\ge 0} \bar{\alpha }_n \bar{\alpha }_m \alpha _k \alpha _l \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hcl≡12∑n+m=k+ln,m,k,l≥0α¯nα¯mαkαlfor complex canonical coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _n(t)$$\end{document}αn(t) whose conjugate momenta are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\bar{\alpha }_n(t)$$\end{document}iα¯n(t), with bars denoting the ordinary complex conjugation. The Hamiltonian (1.2) was introduced, studied and solved in a series of works by Patrick Gérard and Sandrine Grellier [1–4], using position space notation that we shall explain below (as opposed to our mode space notation). We shall be referring to (1.1) and (1.2) as Gérard-Grellier (GG) Hamiltonians.

The main motivation for introducing and studying (1.2) was the topic of turbulent energy transfer from modes with long wavelength (small n) to those with short wavelength (large n). Such turbulent phenomena are an important topic in contemporary PDE mathematics, and are typically challenging to analyze—see [5] for a well-known example where a “weak weak” form of turbulence is established rigorously with considerable effort. The GG Hamiltonian (1.2) plays a very special role in this regard since it displays an array of turbulent phenomena that can be explicitly analyzed using integrability methods. We shall review the classical integrability of (1.2) in Sect. 3 after spelling out in Sect. 2 its place in the broader context of resonant Hamiltonian systems with their connections to weakly nonlinear equations appearing in physics.

While in-depth understanding is available for the integrable structure of the classical Hamiltonian (1.2) with its Lax pairs and hierarchies of conservation laws, this knowledge does not directly transfer to the quantum system, as naive quantization of the classical expressions without special attention to the ordering ambiguities does not recover the conserved charges of the quantum system. Perhaps the most striking feature of the quantum Hamiltonian (1.1), first pointed out in [6], is that its eigenvalue spectrum is made of integers. This feature is beyond any regular expectations, even for a quantum integrable system. Indeed, while a fundamental definition of quantum integrability is lacking [7, 8], the general expectation is that the energy levels of a typical quantum integrable system look like independent random numbers thrown on the real line [9], without level correlations or level repulsion. The perfect integers with rigid level spacings are clearly a different story, and such spectra are typically seen in superintegrable quantum-mechanical systems [10]. In view of this picture, we would like to refer to (1.1) as a superintegrable quantum field theory.

There is more to the story. In what follows, we shall present some approaches to constructing quantum conservation laws. One example is1.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{\min } = \hspace{-2mm}\sum _{\begin{array}{c} n,m,k,l = 1 \\ n+m=k+l \end{array}}^\infty \hspace{-2mm} \min (n,m,k,l) \ a^\dagger _n a^\dagger _{m} a_k a_{l} + \sum _{k=1}^\infty k^2 a^\dagger _k a_k, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hmin=∑n,m,k,l=1n+m=k+l∞min(n,m,k,l)an†am†akal+∑k=1∞k2ak†ak,first pointed out in [11]. This operator commutes with (1.1), and there will be further operators of this sort as well. All the conservation laws we find have purely integer spectra.

In what follows, we shall summarize our current understanding of the quantum Gérard-Grellier Hamiltonian, its hierarchy of conservation laws, and their spectra. This investigation will combine analytic and numerical tools.

The Context of Resonant Hamiltonian Systems

Before focusing on the GG Hamiltonian (1.1), it could be good to step back for a moment and examine the more general system2.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{res}\equiv \frac{1}{2}\sum _{n+m=k+l}^{n,m,k,l\ge 0}C_{nmkl} \bar{\alpha }_n \bar{\alpha }_m \alpha _k \alpha _l \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hres≡12∑n+m=k+ln,m,k,l≥0Cnmklα¯nα¯mαkαlwith arbitrary coupling coefficients C satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{nmkl}=C_{nmlk}=\bar{C}_{klnm}$$\end{document}Cnmkl=Cnmlk=C¯klnm. Evidently, setting C to 1 leads to (1.2), while subsequent canonical quantization leads to (1.1). We refer to such Hamiltonians as resonant Hamiltonians because of the presence of the resonance condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n+m=k+l$$\end{document}n+m=k+l.

Hamiltonian systems of the form (2.1) emerge naturally from weakly nonlinear approximations to Hamiltonian PDEs whose linearized normal modes possess a perfectly resonant, equispaced spectrum of frequencies [12]. Examples include studies of nonlinear Schrödinger equation with harmonic potentials [13–20], which is a model for trapped Bose-Einstein condensates [21], as well as studies of waves in asymptotically anti-de Sitter spacetimes [22–27], with and without gravitational interactions, in relation to the anti-de Sitter instability conjecture of Bizoń and Rostworowski [28]. These systems have also been studied in their own right, mostly as a dynamical arena for turbulent transfer of excitations from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _n$$\end{document}αn with small n to high n (long wavelength to short wavelength in physical settings). This line of research includes the original articles [1–4] that introduced and studied (1.2), as well as [29–35]. Because of the presence of two conservation laws2.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N=\sum _{n=0}^\infty |\alpha _n|^2,\qquad M=\sum _{n=1}^\infty n |\alpha _n|^2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N=∑n=0∞|αn|2,M=∑n=1∞n|αn|2,transfer of excitations to higher n is necessarily accompanied by transfer of excitations to low n, which is known as a ‘dual cascade’ [36], see [34, 35] for analytically tractable examples.

For a quick demonstration of how a Hamiltonian system of the form (2.1) can emerge from a realistic, physical PDE, consider the one-dimensional nonlinear Schrödinger equation with a harmonic potential:2.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} i\,\frac{\partial \Psi }{\partial t}=\frac{1}{2}\left( -\frac{\partial ^2}{\partial x^2}+x^2\right) \Psi +g|\Psi |^2\Psi . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}i∂Ψ∂t=12-∂2∂x2+x2Ψ+g|Ψ|2Ψ.With the nonlinearity turned off (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g=0$$\end{document}g=0), the general solution is2.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Psi =\sum _{n=0}^\infty \alpha _n \psi _n(x) e^{-iE_n t},\qquad E_n=n+\frac{1}{2},\qquad \frac{1}{2}\left( -\frac{\partial ^2}{\partial x^2}+x^2\right) \psi _n=E_n\psi _n, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ψ=∑n=0∞αnψn(x)e-iEnt,En=n+12,12-∂2∂x2+x2ψn=Enψn,with constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _n$$\end{document}αn. At small nonzero coupling g, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _n$$\end{document}αn are no longer constant in time and acquire slow variations. Assuming thus that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _n$$\end{document}αn are functions of time, we can substitute (2.4) into (2.3) to obtain2.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} i \,\frac{d\alpha _n}{dt} = g\sum _{k,l,m=0}^\infty C_{nmkl} \,\bar{\alpha }_m \alpha _k \alpha _l \,e^{i(E_n+E_m-E_k-E_l)t}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}idαndt=g∑k,l,m=0∞Cnmklα¯mαkαlei(En+Em-Ek-El)t,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{nmkl}=\int dx \,\psi _n \psi _m \psi _k \psi _l$$\end{document}Cnmkl=∫dxψnψmψkψl. At small g, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _n$$\end{document}αn vary slowly, while the last exponential factor oscillates on time scales of order 1. It is legitimate to expect that these ‘fast’ oscillations average out and only resonant terms with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_n+E_m-E_k-E_l=n+m-k-l=0$$\end{document}En+Em-Ek-El=n+m-k-l=0 contribute substantially to the evolution. Technically, this provides a good approximation up to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\sim 1/g$$\end{document}t∼1/g (but not on longer timescales). The result of eliminating nonresonant terms is precisely the Hamiltonian equation of motion of (2.1), after g has been absorbed into a redefinition of time. Related mathematically rigorous proofs protecting the accuracy of this resonant approximation can be found in [13, 18]. If one wants to obtain the GG Hamiltonian (1.2) via this resonant approximation process, one has to start with the so-called half-wave equation on a circle [37].

Only special values of the couplings C arise from common physical PDEs, so an interesting question is how to engineer systems that produce desired values of couplings (including the ones we shall focus on in this paper). Ultracold atomic gases in harmonic traps provide a good setting, since harmonic potentials give rise to resonant structures necessary for (2.1). An outstanding problem is, however, to tune the couplings between the normal modes (for instance, by incorporating position-dependent nonlinearities). Another option is waves on a circle, but those waves will have to move in one direction, since the wavenumber index n in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _n$$\end{document}αn is nonnegative. Such ‘chiral waves’ are known to exist as edge states in some condensed matter system [38]. Once again, the interactions of individual normal modes will have to be differentially controlled.

Canonical quantization of (2.1) leads to the corresponding quantum Hamiltonian2.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{quant}= \frac{1}{2}\sum _{n+m=k+l}^{n,m,k,l\ge 0}C_{nmkl} a^\dagger _n a^\dagger _m a_k a_l, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hquant=12∑n+m=k+ln,m,k,l≥0Cnmklan†am†akal,with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[a_n, a_m^\dagger ]=\delta _{mn}$$\end{document}[an,am†]=δmn. This Hamiltonian is strikingly simple to work with, at least at the level of numerical diagonalization, independent of the actual values of the couplings C. Indeed, quantization of (2.2) immediately leads to two quantum conservation laws2.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N=\sum _{n=0}^\infty a^\dagger _n a_n,\qquad M=\sum _{n=1}^\infty n \,a^\dagger _n a_n, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N=∑n=0∞an†an,M=∑n=1∞nan†an,while the space of states is spanned by the Fock vectors: there is one such vector for each set of occupation numbers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _n$$\end{document}ηn, and it is obtained by acting on the vacuum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|0\rangle $$\end{document}|0⟩ (annihilated by all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_n$$\end{document}an) with the creation operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^\dagger _n$$\end{document}an† as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a_0^\dagger )^{\eta _0}(a_1^\dagger )^{\eta _1}\cdots |0\rangle $$\end{document}(a0†)η0(a1†)η1⋯|0⟩. But there is only a finite number of choices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _n$$\end{document}ηn for each N and M (given by the number of integer partitions of M into at most N parts), while all matrix elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{quant}$$\end{document}Hquant between Fock states with different N or M must vanish. For that reason, diagonalization of (2.6) is reduced to diagonalizing an infinite family of finite-sized matrices, which makes the eigenvalues directly accessible [6]. This is in fact how the integer eigenvalues of (1.1) were originally observed. Note that all of this has nothing to do with solvability of (2.1) and (2.6) and the structure is present for any values of the couplings, despite the fact that the corresponding classical dynamics may be arbitrarily complicated (chaotic, turbulent, etc). For special values of C corresponding to quantum integrable systems, as in (1.1), there is much more structure still.

Just like its classical counterpart (2.1), the quantum Hamiltonian (2.6) arises as a weak coupling approximation to certain quantum field theories, and this is even more intuitive than the classical story [39–42]. Indeed, when the classical normal modes have commensurate frequencies, the corresponding quanta have commensurate energies. As a result, quantization of free field theories with highly resonant spectra (nonrelativistic Schrödinger fields in harmonic traps [40, 41], relativistic fields in anti-de Sitter spacetimes [39, 42], etc) leads to highly degenerate multiparticle energy spectra. When weak interactions are turned on, these degenerate levels split, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{quant}$$\end{document}Hquant is the Hamiltonian one needs to diagonalize, in line with the standard Rayleigh–Schrödinger perturbation theory for degenerate spectra, to obtain the energy shifts that split the degenerate noninteracting energy levels. This picture has led to studies of the so-called lowest Landau level Hamiltonian, which belongs to the class (2.6) in relation to the physics of trapped ultracold atomic gases [43–47].

Classical Integrability of the Gérard-Grellier Hamiltonian

Before proceeding to the quantum case (1.1), it would be wise to review the classical integrability theory of (1.2) developed in detail in the original works [1–4]. In doing so, we will also attempt to adapt the presentation for the eyes of physicists.

The equations of motion corresponding to (1.2) read3.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} i\dot{\alpha }_n=\sum _{m=0}^\infty \sum _{k=0}^{n+m} \bar{\alpha }_m\alpha _k\alpha _{n+m-k}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}iα˙n=∑m=0∞∑k=0n+mα¯mαkαn+m-k,where dots will denote time derivatives. For some purposes, it is convenient to rewrite these equations in terms of the position space field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(\theta )$$\end{document}u(θ) on the unit circle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in [0,2\pi )$$\end{document}θ∈[0,2π):3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u(\theta ,t)\equiv \sum _{n=0}^\infty e^{in\theta }\alpha _n(t), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u(θ,t)≡∑n=0∞einθαn(t),which yields3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} i\dot{u}=\Pi (|u|^2u), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}iu˙=Π(|u|2u),with3.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Pi \left( \sum _{n=-\infty }^\infty h_n e^{in\theta }\right) \equiv \sum _{n=0}^\infty h_n e^{in\theta } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Π∑n=-∞∞hneinθ≡∑n=0∞hneinθbeing the Szegő projector. In (3.3), one can recognize the original cubic Szegő equation as introduced in [1]. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(\theta )$$\end{document}u(θ) naturally extends to the complex plane by writing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(z)=\sum _{n=0}^\infty z^n\alpha _n(t)$$\end{document}u(z)=∑n=0∞znαn(t), in which case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(z=e^{i\theta })$$\end{document}u(z=eiθ) recovers the original definition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(\theta )$$\end{document}u(θ). Many statements about the classical system are proved very effectively in this position space picture using properties of Szegő projectors, but in preparation for analyzing the quantum system, we will often stress the mode space representation, since, once quantized, the complex amplitudes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _n$$\end{document}αn turn into a conventional creation-annihilation operator algebra, while the corresponding field operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(\theta )$$\end{document}u(θ) acquire rather unusual nonlocal commutation relations.

It was recognized early on [1] that (3.3) is a very special equation and in particular, it admits an infinite hierarchy of dynamically invariant manifolds formed by meromorphic u(z) with a fixed number of poles in the complex plane. If the initial conditions are of this form, the evolution described by (3.3) will amount to moving the positions of the poles and changing their residues, but the number of singularities will not change. The poles thus act as solitons. In the mode space language, the invariant manifolds are defined by configurations of the form3.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha _n(t)=\sum _{r=1}^R c_r(t)\,[p_r(t)]^n \end{aligned}$$\end{document}αn(t)=∑r=1Rcr(t)[pr(t)]nat some prescribed value of R. Further inquiry into the structure of (3.3) has led to the discovery of its Lax pairs that we will briefly summarize immediately below. The original presentation of [1] revolves around the antilinear operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_u h=\Pi (u\bar{h})$$\end{document}Huh=Π(uh¯), resulting in a Lax pair in which the first Lax operator is antilinear. This is used very wisely in the constructions of [1], but as it is rather unconventional, we will rely in our presentation on the linear Lax operator obtained by squaring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_u$$\end{document}Hu.

We proceed with presenting the Lax pair in the mode space language of (1.2). To this end, we introduce auxiliary ‘test’ vectors in the mode space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vec {h}=(h_0,h_1,\cdots )$$\end{document}h→=(h0,h1,⋯), and also, for future use, the special vector3.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \vec {1}=(1,0,0,\cdots ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1→=(1,0,0,⋯).The classical Lax pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {L},\mathcal {M})$$\end{document}(L,M) is defined by its action on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vec {h}$$\end{document}h→ as3.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\mathcal {L}\vec {h})_n=\sum _{k,l=0}^\infty \alpha _{n+k}\bar{\alpha }_{k+l} h_l ,\qquad (\mathcal {M}\vec {h})_n=\sum _{m+p\ge n}^{m,p\ge 0}\alpha _p\bar{\alpha (There is an elegant proof of this statement in [1] using Szegő projectors, but for completeness, we provide, in Appendix A, a brute force proof in the mode space language used here.) This has all the usual consequences of the Lax theory, in particular, the conservation of an infinite tower of charges given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Tr}[\mathcal {L}^p]$$\end{document}Tr[Lp] for any p, whose time derivatives vanish by (3.8) and the cyclic property of the trace. But there is much more structure in this particular case than what is dictated by the general Lax theory.

One special property of the Lax operators (3.7) is3.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}\vec {1}=\mathcal {M}\vec {1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L1→=M1→.As a result, one gets an extra Lax pair that provides an extension of the Lax structure (3.8). There is another Lax-pair construction of a similar sort. This is much more than what one expects in general from a Lax-integrable system. Lax-integrable deformations of the cubic Szegő equation have been constructed [29, 30, 33], though only part of the analytic structure presented here survives when the equation has been deformed. Finally, we comment on the Lax pair corresponding to the classical evolution defined by (3.16), which we found in the course of our investigations. The operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}$$\end{document}L of (3.7) remains unchanged, while its Lax partner \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}$$\end{document}M must be replaced with3.23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\mathcal {M}\vec {h})_n = \sum _{m+p\ge n}^{m,p\ge 0} n \, \alpha _p\bar{\alpha }_{m+p-n} h_m - \hspace{-0.3cm }\sum ^{ \min (n,m)\ge p}_{m,p\ge 0} \hspace{-0.2cm }p \, \alpha _{n-p}\bar{\alpha }_{m-p} h_m - \hspace{-0.1cm }\sum _{ n+p\ge m}^{m \ge n} (m-n) \, \alpha _p\bar{\alpha }_{n+p-m} h_m \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Mh→)n=∑m+p≥nm,p≥0nαpα¯m+p-nhm-∑m,p≥0min(n,m)≥ppαn-pα¯m-phm-∑n+p≥mm≥n(m-n)αpα¯n+p-mhmin order to accommodate the evolution generated by the Hamiltonian (3.16).

The Quantum Gérard-Grellier Hamiltonian

We finally return to the quantum Hamiltonian (1.1), which will be the main subject of our study for the rest of this paper. As explained at the end of Sect. 2, the space of states of this Hamiltonian is spanned by the Fock vectors\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\eta _0,\eta _1,\cdots \rangle&\equiv \left( \prod _{k=0}^\infty \frac{(a^\dagger _k)^{\eta _k}}{\sqrt{\eta _k!}}\right) |0,0,\cdots \rangle , \\&a_k |0,0,\cdots \rangle =0,\quad a_k^\dagger a_k|\eta _0,\eta _1,\cdots \rangle =\eta _k|\eta _0,\eta _1,\cdots \rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|η0,η1,⋯⟩≡∏k=0∞(ak†)ηkηk!|0,0,⋯⟩,ak|0,0,⋯⟩=0,ak†ak|η0,η1,⋯⟩=ηk|η0,η1,⋯⟩.Because the operators N and M defined in (2.7) commute with the Hamiltonian, the latter can only have nonvanishing matrix elements between the Fock states with the same values of N (given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _k\eta _k$$\end{document}∑kηk) and M (given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _k k\eta _k$$\end{document}∑kkηk). But since there is only a finite number of such states within each (N, M)-block, one is left with independent finite-sized matrices. Diagonalizing these finite-sized matrices reveals integer eigenvalues, providing thereby an entry point [6] to the manifold analytic puzzles presented by (1.1). What kind of structure can we expect to underlie these patterns, also in view of the classical integrability properties reviewed in Sect. 3?

The integer eigenvalues suggest that ladder operators must be present that convert eigenvectors to eigenvectors while changing the eigenvalues by integer shifts. We will indeed report some such operators at the end of this section, and more in Sect. 9.

Quantization of the hierarchies of conservation laws given by (3.14) and (3.15) in the classical theory is a very natural outstanding question. Naive quantization based on replacing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_n$$\end{document}α¯n with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_n^\dagger $$\end{document}an† and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _n$$\end{document}αn with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_n$$\end{document}an fails for any obvious ordering prescription. For example, we could try to keep the order as in (3.14) and (3.15), or we could try to impose normal ordering, with all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^\dagger $$\end{document}a†’s moved to the left and all a’s moved to the right. None of these prescriptions produce valid quantum conservation laws that commute with (1.1). In fact, we know that the classical conservation law \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_2$$\end{document}G2 can be rewritten in terms of a structure identical to the first term of the valid quantum conservation law (1.3). (We will provide a brute force proof that (1.3) commutes with (1.1) in Sect. 6.) This makes us expect that other conservation laws will behave similarly: they will consist of the highest-order polynomial term that can be visualized as the corresponding classical conservation law with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\bar{\alpha }_n,\alpha _n)$$\end{document}(α¯n,αn) replaced with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a_n^\dagger ,a_n)$$\end{document}(an†,an), normal-ordered, plus lower-order polynomial quantum corrections. This is precisely the structure of (1.3), and it will be true of all other conservation laws we can explicitly construct. What is even more striking is that, for all quantum conservation laws we construct, we empirically find purely integer eigenvalue spectra. This will be reported in Sect. 12, together with our knowledge about the rather peculiar quantum Lax pair, also obtained by introducing a quantum correction to the classical Lax pair (3.7).

Before proceeding with more in-depth studies of the GG Hamiltonian, it will be handy to assemble here a few useful operators and state the algebra they form. In much of our exposition, we will keep in the back of our mind the picture of diagonalizing simultaneously H given by (1.1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin given by (1.3). First, this will remove some of the possible degeneracies present when H is diagonalized by itself. Second, there are nice algebraic relations that give this simultaneous diagonalization process a useful purpose.

We first remark that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0^\dagger $$\end{document}a0† commutes with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin, since the latter only involves modes number 1 and higher. For that reason, for any eigenvector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\Psi \rangle $$\end{document}|Ψ⟩ of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin in block (N, M), we can take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0^\dagger |\Psi \rangle $$\end{document}a0†|Ψ⟩ in block \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(N+1,M)$$\end{document}(N+1,M) and it will be an eigenvector of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin as well. Furthermore, we can introduce quantum shift operators S and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^\dagger $$\end{document}S† analogous to (3.21):4.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S|\eta _0,\eta _1,\cdots \rangle =|0,\eta _0,\eta _1,\cdots \rangle ,\qquad S^\dagger |\eta _0,\eta _1,\eta _2\cdots \rangle =|\eta _1,\eta _2\cdots \rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S|η0,η1,⋯⟩=|0,η0,η1,⋯⟩,S†|η0,η1,η2⋯⟩=|η1,η2⋯⟩.We define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^\dagger $$\end{document}S† to annihilate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\eta _0,\eta _1,\cdots \rangle $$\end{document}|η0,η1,⋯⟩ unless \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _0=0$$\end{document}η0=0. With this, we have4.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S^\dagger S = 1,\qquad SS^\dagger = P_{0}, \qquad P_0S=S,\qquad S^\dagger P_0=S^\dagger , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S†S=1,SS†=P0,P0S=S,S†P0=S†,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{0}$$\end{document}P0 is the projector onto the null space of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0$$\end{document}a0, as well as the following useful relations:4.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S M S^\dagger&= M-N, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}SMS†=M-N,4.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S^\dagger H S&= H, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S†HS=H,4.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{\min } S&= S(2H+H_{\min } + 2M+N). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HminS=S(2H+Hmin+2M+N).The last relation means that, if we have simultaneously diagonalized H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin in block (N, M), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\Psi \rangle $$\end{document}|Ψ⟩ is one of their joint eigenvectors, the vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S|\Psi \rangle $$\end{document}S|Ψ⟩ residing in the block \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(N,M+N)$$\end{document}(N,M+N) is an eigenvector of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin with eigenvalue determined by (4.5).

The above picture suggests a recursive process for diagonalizing H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin: imagine we have already diagonalized these two operators in the blocks whose values of N or M are below the current block (N, M). Then, take the eigenvectors from the block \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(k,M-k)$$\end{document}(k,M-k), denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{k,M-k}$$\end{document}Vk,M-k, and transport them to the block (k, M) with S. In order to reach the block (N, M), one can apply \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0^\dagger $$\end{document}a0† acting with it \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N-k$$\end{document}N-k times. We obtain groups of (mutually) linearly independent vectors, all of which are eigenvectors of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin. Furthermore, the disjoint union of these vectors4.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigcup _{k=1}^{\min (N,M)} \frac{a_0^{\dagger N-k}}{\sqrt{(N-k)!}} S V_{k,M-k} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⋃k=1min(N,M)a0†N-k(N-k)!SVk,M-kforms a complete basis in the block (N, M). This follows directly from the linear independence of these states, combined with a counting argument relating the dimensions of the spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{k,M-k}$$\end{document}Vk,M-k and the size of the block (N, M). The number of vectors in an (N, M) block is the number of integer partitions of M into at most N parts [6] denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_N(M)$$\end{document}pN(M). This number-theoretic function satisfies the recursion relation4.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_N(M)=p_{N-1}(M)+p_N(M-N). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pN(M)=pN-1(M)+pN(M-N).(A partition of M into at most N parts is either a partition into exactly N parts, so that no parts are zero and we can subtract 1 from each part, obtaining a partition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M-N$$\end{document}M-N into at most N parts, or it is a partition of M into at most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N-1$$\end{document}N-1 part.) Applying this relation recursively to the first term on the right-hand side of (4.7) yields4.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_N(M)= \sum _{k=1}^{\min (N,M)}p_{k}(M-k) \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pN(M)=∑k=1min(N,M)pk(M-k).This shows that (4.6) contains precisely enough linearly independent vectors to be a basis for the block (N, M). We have thus obtained, by this very simple process, an eigenbasis of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin in the current block. The only issue is that, to restart the iterative procedure we need a joint eigenbasis of H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin, which may require re-diagonalization within the degenerate subspaces of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin. So we must inspect the spectrum and rediagonalize H within any such degenerate subspaces.

We will see in Sects. 8 and 10 that, in application to the two highest subspaces of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin, this procedure gives an explicit construction of families of eigenvectors. The entry point of this construction is the joint top eigenvectors of H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin that we will build in Sect. 7. There is a relation between these top eigenspaces of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin and classical invariant manifolds (3.5), as we shall explain in Sect. 11. Before proceeding with that, we will construct, in Sect. 5, bounds on H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin that will help us identify these top subspaces. For future use we define, in addition to the operators introduced above, the following family of operators4.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J_1\equiv a_0,\qquad J_{2n+1}=[J_{2n-1},H]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J1≡a0,J2n+1=[J2n-1,H].These are quantum analogs of the classical quantities with the same name in [1].

Energy Bounds

The bounds on H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin will play an essential role in our constructions of explicit families of eigenvectors below. They are also important more broadly in controlling the spectral properties of H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin, and will reveal useful algebraic structures. We will use such subspaces defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{js\alpha }|\Psi \rangle =0$$\end{document}Bjsα|Ψ⟩=0 for diagonalizing H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin.

In (5.1), we used a complete-basis decompositions of quadratic forms, ending up with somewhat awkward expressions in terms of the eigenvectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_k$$\end{document}ek. The index range conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_1$$\end{document}H1 is purely quartic, in contrast with (1.3) that has a quadratic ‘quantum’ piece. Viewed from the vantage point of the above formulas, this quadratic piece has a trivial origin: it comes from normal-ordering \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^2$$\end{document}M2. We will rely on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_0$$\end{document}H0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_1$$\end{document}H1 in the next section, and prove that they commute, which will automatically imply that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[H,H_{\min }]=0$$\end{document}[H,Hmin]=0.

We remark that sum-of-squares decompositions like (5.19) and (5.20) are very typical of the ‘factorization method’ and supersymmetric quantum mechanics [48]. However, since D and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D^\dagger $$\end{document}D† do not commute for different index values, it is not immediately obvious how to use them for constructing a full solution.

A Brute Force Proof that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {[}\! H,H_{\min }\text {]}\ \text {=}\ 0$$\end{document}[H,Hmin]=0

Since we use simultaneous diagonalization of H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin in our constructions of the eigenvectors below, the property \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[H,H_{\min }]=0$$\end{document}[H,Hmin]=0 is essential for our considerations. The Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin was originally guessed in [11] as an outcome of a numerical procedure that algorithmically constructs conservation laws polynomial in the creation-annihilation operators as explicit matrices within the individual (N, M)-blocks of the Hilbert space. It is easy to verify within any given (N, M)-block that the two operators indeed commute, but what we need is a general analytic proof.

While we expect that, eventually, a systematic understanding of the conservation laws of H will emerge in the spirit of Lax theory, we are far from that point, and a practical solution is to provide a brute force proof that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[H,H_{\min }]=0$$\end{document}[H,Hmin]=0. It is more convenient to prove instead that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[H_0,H_1]=0$$\end{document}[H0,H1]=0, with the definitions (5.19) and (5.20), which would automatically imply \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[H,H_{\min }]=0$$\end{document}[H,Hmin]=0 in view of (5.22). We provide a proof below. It is essential for our subsequent considerations, but rather bulky and tedious. The rest of our treatment will only use the final result, but not the details of this proof, so the details can be freely skipped.

We remark that we could in principle throw away the top polynomial piece of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[H_0,H_1]$$\end{document}[H0,H1] after normal ordering, because that piece must agree with the classical computation, and in the classical theory, H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin Poisson-commute from the general Lax theory described in Sect. 3. We opt, however, for a completely mechanical proof that follows through detailed bookkeeping of all the terms emerging from the commutation and proceeds to demonstrate that the result is zero.

We introduce the shorthand \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{\{jkl\}}\equiv \sum _{j=0}^\infty \sum _{k,l=0}^j$$\end{document}∑{jkl}≡∑j=0∞∑k,l=0j.
