Relation to Classical Invariant Manifolds

The families of eigenstates derived above are reminiscent of the integrable sectors in the resonant systems studied in [41, 42]. These systems were previously shown to have a single (three-dimensional) invariant manifold [15, 16, 27, 31] and the exactly solvable set of Hamiltonian eigenstates can be combined into coherent-like superpositions that reproduce the classical dynamics in the semiclassical limit [41, 42]. This connection between special subsets of Hamiltonian eigenstates and classical dynamically invariant manifolds can furthermore be effectively visualized using phase space techniques [50]. There is a considerable resemblance between this story outlined in [41, 42] and the special families of eigenvectors of the GG Hamiltonian constructed above, hence one may expect the eigenstates of H to include sectors that encode the classical dynamics within the invariant manifolds (3.5).

The simplest correspondence of this sort is the relation between the top eigenstates of H within each (N, M)-block and the ansatz (3.5) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R=1$$\end{document}R=1. To see this, we examine the expectation values of the mode occupation number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^\dagger _n a_n$$\end{document}an†an within these states, which is an analog of the classical observable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\alpha _n|^2$$\end{document}|αn|2. For the highest energy state (7.5), one can show that11.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a_n |\psi ^{\max }_{N,M} \rangle = \sqrt{N(N-1)}\,\,\sqrt{\frac{M!(N+M-n-2)!}{(M-n)!(N+M-1)!}}\,\,|\psi ^{\max }_{N-1,M-n} \rangle \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}an|ψN,Mmax⟩=N(N-1)M!(N+M-n-2)!(M-n)!(N+M-1)!|ψN-1,M-nmax⟩.In the semiclassical limit, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N,M \gg n $$\end{document}N,M≫n are large while keeping their ratio \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d^2 \equiv M/N$$\end{document}d2≡M/N fixed, one then gets11.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \psi ^{\max }_{N,M} | a^\dagger _n a_n |\psi ^{\max }_{N,M} \rangle = \frac{N}{1+d^2}\left( \frac{d^2}{1+d^2}\right) ^n . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨ψN,Mmax|an†an|ψN,Mmax⟩=N1+d2d21+d2n.This has exactly the same form as the observable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\alpha _n|^2$$\end{document}|αn|2 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _n$$\end{document}αn within the ansatz (3.5) at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R=1$$\end{document}R=1. The energy of the solutions in this lowest manifold, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}(1)$$\end{document}M(1) in [1], likewise saturates the classical energy bound [1].

We expect this connection with classical invariant manifolds to extend to more complicated families of eigenvectors, though we will not explore this in detail here. To clarify our intuition, we recall that, according to [1], in addition to the invariant manifolds (3.5), denoted there \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}(R)$$\end{document}M(R), one has the invariant manifolds11.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha _n(t)=c(t)\,\delta _{n,0}+\sum _{r=1}^R c_r(t)\,[p_r(t)]^n, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}αn(t)=c(t)δn,0+∑r=1Rcr(t)[pr(t)]n,denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\mathcal {M}}(R)$$\end{document}M~(R). There is an interlacing embedding of these manifolds into each other. The manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}(R)$$\end{document}M(R) is evidently a submanifold of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\mathcal {M}}(R)$$\end{document}M~(R) obtained by setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c=0$$\end{document}c=0. On the other hand, the manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\mathcal {M}}(R)$$\end{document}M~(R) is a submanifold of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}(R+1)$$\end{document}M(R+1) obtained by setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{R+1}=0$$\end{document}pR+1=0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_{R+1}=c$$\end{document}cR+1=c. The action of the classical shift operator (3.21) moves configurations up this invariant manifold ladder. Namely, if we apply the shift to a configuration (3.5), the result still satisfies (3.5), with redefined \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_r$$\end{document}cr and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_r$$\end{document}pr, except for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=0$$\end{document}n=0, since now \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _0=0$$\end{document}α0=0. In other words, we obtain a configuration that fits the ansatz (3.5), except for the shifted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _0$$\end{document}α0. But this is precisely the definition (11.3), so the configuration is in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\mathcal {M}}(R)$$\end{document}M~(R). Furthermore, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\mathcal {M}}(R)\in \mathcal {M}(R+1)$$\end{document}M~(R)∈M(R+1), subsequent action of the shift operator will push the configuration to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\mathcal {M}}(R+1)$$\end{document}M~(R+1) and so on.

As explained above, the top eigenstates correspond to configurations in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}(1)$$\end{document}M(1)—more precisely, one will have to take superpositions of the top eigenstates in different nearby blocks to obtain a state localized around a specific classical configuration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _n=cp^n$$\end{document}αn=cpn. We have furthermore been repeatedly obtaining other families of eigenvectors by acting on these top vectors (and their descendants) with the quantum shift operator (4.1). This strongly suggests that these lower families correspond to higher invariant manifolds described above. One would need, however, a more thorough and systematic understanding of the descendant eigenstate families to explore this correspondence comprehensively.

Quantum Lax Pair and Conservation Laws

Quantum Lax pairs [51] are not a very common subject for discussion, since in general, they are both difficult to construct because of operator ordering problems and do not provide the same empowerment of the formalism and automatic construction of conservation laws as in the corresponding classical theory. They have found, however, some applications to quantum Calogero systems [52], see [53, 54].

For the quantum GG Hamiltonian, we have been able to identify a curious Lax pair structure that we shall describe here. As in the classical case, introduce an auxiliary space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vec {h}\equiv (h_0,h_1,\ldots )$$\end{document}h→≡(h0,h1,…) of number-valued vectors in the mode space. The Lax operators will be infinite matrices acting on these vectors, while the entries of these matrices will be Hilbert space operators made of a and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^\dagger $$\end{document}a†. The specific expressions are12.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\mathcal {L}\vec {h})_n=\sum _{k,l=0}^\infty a_{l+k}^\dagger a_{k+n} h_l +nh_n,\qquad (\mathcal {M}\vec {h})_n=\sum _{l+m\ge n}^{l,m\ge 0}a_{l+m-n}^\dagger a_lh_m. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Lh→)n=∑k,l=0∞al+k†ak+nhl+nhn,(Mh→)n=∑l+m≥nl,m≥0al+m-n†alhm.Except for the second ‘quantum’ term in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}$$\end{document}L, this replicates the structure of the classical Lax pair (3.7). Introducing the identity matrix I in the space of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vec {h}$$\end{document}h→, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\vec {h}=\vec {h}$$\end{document}Ih→=h→, the evolution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}$$\end{document}L is12.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} i \dot{\mathcal {L}} = [\mathcal {L},H I] \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}iL˙=[L,HI],and the Lax pair condition can be written simply as12.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}HI+\mathcal {M},\mathcal {L}]=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[HI+M,L]=0.To prove by brute force that (12.3) is valid, we can evaluate the commutators directly and then rewrite the result in terms of normal-ordered operators. The top-order piece, which is quartic in the creation-annihilation operators has to vanish by the classical computation in Appendix A, since all the terms are normal-ordered, no commutations of a and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^\dagger $$\end{document}a† are necessary, and the classical and quantum computations will agree line-by-line. The remainder consists of two quadratic contributions from12.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} ([\mathcal {M},\mathcal {L}]h)_n =&\sum _{l+m\ge n}^{k,k',l,m\ge 0} a_{l+m-n}^\dagger a_l a_{k'+k}^\dagger a_{k+m} h_{k'} +\sum _{l+m\ge n}^{l,m\ge 0} m\,a_{l+m-n}^\dagger a_lh_m \nonumber \\&-\sum _{k'+m\ge l}^{k,k',m,l\ge 0} a_{l+k}^\dagger a_{k+n} a_{k'+m-l}^\dagger a_{k'}h_m -n\sum _{l+m\ge n}^{l,m\ge 0}a_{l+m-n}^\dagger a_lh_m. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}([M,L]h)n=∑l+m≥nk,k′,l,m≥0al+m-n†alak′+k†ak+mhk′+∑l+m≥nl,m≥0mal+m-n†alhm-∑k′+m≥lk,k′,m,l≥0al+k†ak+nak′+m-l†ak′hm-n∑l+m≥nl,m≥0al+m-n†alhm.First, there are terms that emerge from bringing the quartic contributions to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mathcal {M},\mathcal {L}]$$\end{document}[M,L] into a normal-ordered form; second, there are terms arising from the quantum piece \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$nh_n$$\end{document}nhn in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}$$\end{document}L-operator of (12.1). We must show that these two quadratic pieces cancel each other. We consider the quartic contributions from the first and third terms in (12.4) in turn. The first term gives the following quadratic contribution:12.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\sum _{l+m\ge n}^{k,k',l,m\ge 0} a_{l+m-n}^\dagger a_l a_{k'+k}^\dagger a_{k+m} h_{k'} \nonumber \\&\quad \rightarrow \sum _{l+m\ge n}^{k,k',l,m\ge 0} a_{l+m-n}^\dagger \delta _{l,k'+k} a_{k+m} h_{k'} = \sum _{k+k'+m\ge n}^{k,k',m\ge 0} a_{k+k'+m-n}^\dagger a_{k+m} h_{k'} \nonumber \\&\quad = \sum _{\begin{array}{c} k'+j\ge n\\ j\ge m \end{array}}^{j,k',m\ge 0} a_{k'+j-n}^\dagger a_{j} h_{k'} = \sum _{k'+j\ge n}^{j,k'\ge 0} j \, a_{k'+j-n}^\dagger a_{j} h_{k'} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑l+m≥nk,k′,l,m≥0al+m-n†alak′+k†ak+mhk′→∑l+m≥nk,k′,l,m≥0al+m-n†δl,k′+kak+mhk′=∑k+k′+m≥nk,k′,m≥0ak+k′+m-n†ak+mhk′=∑k′+j≥nj≥mj,k′,m≥0ak′+j-n†ajhk′=∑k′+j≥nj,k′≥0jak′+j-n†ajhk′.First, we remove the sum over l using the Kronecker \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ, then we introduce the index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j=k+m$$\end{document}j=k+m to swap the sum over k for a sum over j. The label m no longer appears in the summand, and therefore the respective sum is straightforwardly computed. We can perform similar steps for the third term in (12.4):12.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{k'+m\ge l}^{k,k',m,l\ge 0} a_{l+k}^\dagger a_{k+n} a_{k'+m-l}^\dagger a_{k'}h_m&\rightarrow \sum _{k'+m\ge l}^{k,k',m,l\ge 0} a_{l+k}^\dagger \delta _{k+n,k'+m-l}a_{k'}h_m = \sum _{\begin{array}{c} k'+m\ge n \\ k'+m\ge n+k \end{array}}^{k,k',m\ge 0} a_{k'+m-n}^\dagger a_{k'}h_m \nonumber \\&= \sum _{k'+m\ge n}^{k',m\ge 0} (k'+m-n)a_{k'+m-n}^\dagger a_{k'}h_m , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑k′+m≥lk,k′,m,l≥0al+k†ak+nak′+m-l†ak′hm→∑k′+m≥lk,k′,m,l≥0al+k†δk+n,k′+m-lak′hm=∑k′+m≥nk′+m≥n+kk,k′,m≥0ak′+m-n†ak′hm=∑k′+m≥nk′,m≥0(k′+m-n)ak′+m-n†ak′hm,where we performed the sum over l and this eliminated all dependence on the label k in the summand. Subtracting (12.6) from (12.5), the remaining terms precisely cancel the second and fourth contributions in (12.4). We have therefore explicitly shown that (12.3) holds at the quantum level, with the modified Lax pair (12.1).

It is usually impossible to directly construct quantum conservation laws from such quantum Lax pairs as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Tr}\mathcal {L}^n$$\end{document}TrLn, since the lack of commutativity of Hilbert space operators upsets the cyclic property of the trace [51]. However, in our case, there is a trick that closely replicates the construction of classical conservation laws in (3.14) originally discovered in [1], which is different from the usual trace-based construction. Namely, we first introduce the vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vec {1}\equiv (1,0,0,0,\ldots )$$\end{document}1→≡(1,0,0,0,…) and notice the identity12.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}\vec {1}=\mathcal {M}\vec {1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L1→=M1→.Then we introduce the operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\vec {1},\mathcal {L}^n\vec {1})$$\end{document}(1→,Ln1→) and observe that they commute with the Hamiltonian because12.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}H,(\vec {1},\mathcal {L}^n\vec {1})]=(\vec {1},[HI,\mathcal {L}^n]\vec {1})=(\vec {1},(\mathcal {L}^n\mathcal {M}-\mathcal {M}\mathcal {L}^n)\vec {1})=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[H,(1→,Ln1→)]=(1→,[HI,Ln]1→)=(1→,(LnM-MLn)1→)=0.As the operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\vec {1},\mathcal {L}^n\vec {1})$$\end{document}(1→,Ln1→) formally commute with the Hamiltonian, they provide an infinite family of conservation laws. There is unfortunately a complication with this construction, however, that precludes its immediate utilization for practical purposes. The operators obtained in this manner are not normal-ordered, and attempting to normal-order them, one gets contributions in the form of lower order polynomials in a and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^\dagger $$\end{document}a† with infinite coefficients. It is natural to expect that these infinities are themselves lower-order conservation laws and may be systematically subtracted, yielding finite, well-defined conserved operators. Namely, one can truncate all sums at a finite large mode number and add a combination of lower-order conservation laws with divergent coefficients depending on this cutoff. With suitable finetuning, the total expression will have a well-defined limit as the cutoff is removed, providing a conservation law. We have verified using computer algebra that this can be done for the first few members of the tower. (We found the FORM software [55] useful for this purpose, due to its built-in ability to effectively handle large polynomial expressions, possibly made of noncommuting variables.) We do not have, however, a systematic theory of such subtractions, and hence no way to employ the Lax pair (12.1) to construct conservation laws effectively.

In the course of our numerical experimentation, we have come across another construction of conservation laws that appears to work at all orders. The formula is very simple and gives the conserved quantities as12.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J_{2n} = [ J_{2n+1},a_0^\dagger ], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J2n=[J2n+1,a0†],where the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_{2n+1}$$\end{document}J2n+1 is defined recursively by (4.9). Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_2$$\end{document}J2 is related to N, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_4$$\end{document}J4 is related to H. The higher J’s are independent polynomial conservation laws. To illustrate the sort of quantum corrections that are generated by (12.9), we provide an explicit expression for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_6$$\end{document}J6:12.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J_6&= 2 \hspace{-0.3cm}\sum _{k, l, m,n,p=0}^\infty \hspace{-0.3cm}a^\dagger _{k} a^\dagger _{l+m} a^\dagger _{n+p} a_{k+l} a_{m+n} a_p +2 \hspace{-0.1cm}\sum _{k, l, m=0}^\infty (k+l+m) \, a^\dagger _{k} a^\dagger _{l+m} a_{k+l} a_{m} \nonumber \\&\hspace{4cm}+2 H_{\min }+ (2N+6)H + N^3 + N^2+2 M N + 2 M . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J6=2∑k,l,m,n,p=0∞ak†al+m†an+p†ak+lam+nap+2∑k,l,m=0∞(k+l+m)ak†al+m†ak+lam+2Hmin+(2N+6)H+N3+N2+2MN+2M.One can, of course, throw away the second line since it is expressed through the lower conservation laws. The sextic piece in the first line matches the structure of the classical conserved quantities (3.14), but there is a nontrivial quartic correction.

We have not been able to prove analytically that (12.9) are conserved, though we have run extensive checks that the corresponding matrices commute with the Hamiltonian within individual (N, M)-blocks. It is not difficult to understand, however, why the corresponding classical quantities are conserved as they are related to the classical tower (3.14). This follows immediately from the expression for the classical quantities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^{cl}_{2n+1}\equiv i\left\{ J^{cl}_{2n-1},H_{cl}\right\} $$\end{document}J2n+1cl≡iJ2n-1cl,Hcl corresponding to the operators (4.9), with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^{cl}_1\equiv \alpha _0$$\end{document}J1cl≡α0. In terms of the classical modes, they are12.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J^{cl}_{2n+1}= \hspace{-3mm}\sum _{i_1,\ldots , i_{2n}=0}^\infty \hspace{-3mm}\alpha _{i_1}\bar{\alpha }_{i_1+i_2} \alpha _{i_2+i_3}\cdots \bar{\alpha }_{i_{2n-1}+i_{2n}} \alpha _{i_{2n}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J2n+1cl=∑i1,…,i2n=0∞αi1α¯i1+i2αi2+i3⋯α¯i2n-1+i2nαi2n.Then, one straightforwardly finds the Poisson bracket12.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} i\left\{ J^{cl}_{2n+1} , \bar{\alpha }_0\right\} = \sum _{n=0}^{\infty } \frac{\partial J^{cl}_{2n+1}}{\partial \alpha _n} \frac{\partial \bar{\alpha }_0}{\partial \bar{\alpha }_n} = \sum _{i=0}^{n} I_{n-i}I_i \end{aligned}$$\end{document}iJ2n+1cl,α¯0=∑n=0∞∂J2n+1cl∂αn∂α¯0∂α¯n=∑i=0nIn-iIiwith \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_0 \equiv 1$$\end{document}I0≡1, which is indeed conserved. It is remarkable that applying the correspondence principle to the equivalent representation (12.12) of the tower (3.20) to obtain (12.9) leads to quantum conservation laws without running into operator ordering issues.

Additionally, one expects an extra quantum tower related to the classical traces of powers of the Lax operator given by (3.15). That tower starts with M of (2.7), and we also know its quartic member (1.3) that we discovered empirically in [11] and also provided a brute force proof that it is conserved in Sect. 6. Additionally, we have recovered by trial-and-error as well as guided by the corresponding classical conservation law \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_3$$\end{document}G3 of (3.15) the following sextic conserved operator:12.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{t,n,l,m,k,p,=0}^\infty a^\dagger _{t+n}a^\dagger _{l+m}a^\dagger _{k+p}a_{n+l}a_{m+k}a_{p+t} + 6 \sum _{t,n,l,m=0}^\infty t a^\dagger _{t+n}a^\dagger _{l+m}a_{n+l}a_{m+t} + \sum _{n=0}^\infty n^3 a^\dagger _{n}a_{n}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑t,n,l,m,k,p,=0∞at+n†al+m†ak+p†an+lam+kap+t+6∑t,n,l,m=0∞tat+n†al+m†an+lam+t+∑n=0∞n3an†an.We do not know how to extend this tower to higher orders, nor is it clear what relation it bears to the quantum Lax pair (12.1).

Finally, perhaps the most remarkable property of all, which we once again only know from numerical observations, is that all eigenvalues of the conservation laws are integers, not only for H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\min }$$\end{document}Hmin, but also for (12.9) and (12.13).

Parallels with Calogero and Benjamin-Ono Systems

The presence of integer eigenvalues for the GG Hamiltonian and its partner conservation laws alludes to the structures typical of Calogero systems [52–54], the simplest of which describes N particles on a line with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/r^2$$\end{document}1/r2 two-body interactions:13.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{Calogero}=\sum _{i=1}^N \frac{p_i^2}{2}+\sum _{i<j}\frac{g}{(x_i-x_j)^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HCalogero=∑i=1Npi22+∑i<jg(xi-xj)2.Even beyond the mere observation of integer eigenvalues, the peculiar construction of conservation laws in (3.14) that does not utilize traces finds its parallels in considerations of Calogero particles [54]. Despite these intriguing similarities, the GG Hamiltonian is in the form of a field theory rather than a quantum-mechanical many-body system, while straightforward second quantization of the Calogero system does not produce anything resembling the GG Hamiltonian [56].

More practical parallels can be seen between the structures revealed by our present exposition and the quantum Benjamin-Ono system [57, 58]. At the classical level, similarity of integrability structures of the Benjamin-Ono and cubic Szegő equations has been revealed in [59, 60] and has led to an explicit analytic solution of the Benjamin-Ono equation analogous to (3.22) – see [61, 62] for further generalizations. The quantum Benjamin-Ono Hamiltonian defined in [57] can be recast in a notation compatible with (1.1) as13.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{qBO}=\sum _{m,n=1}^\infty \sqrt{\alpha ^3 \,mn(m+n)}\left( a^\dagger _m a^\dagger _n a_{m+n}+a^\dagger _{m+n}a_na_m\right) +\alpha (\alpha -1)\sum _{n=1}^\infty n^2a^\dagger _n a_n, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HqBO=∑m,n=1∞α3mn(m+n)am†an†am+n+am+n†anam+α(α-1)∑n=1∞n2an†an,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α is a parameter. (Compared to [57], we have changed the normalization of the creation-annihilation operators in order to give them standard commutation relations.) This Hamiltonian conserves M defined by (2.7), but not the particle number N. It possesses a quantum Lax pair [57] leading to a construction of conservation laws reminiscent of (12.8), without encountering the divergences seen in the previous section for the GG Hamiltonian.

A complete solution for the joint eigenstates of the Hamiltonian (13.2) and its hierarchy of conservation laws was given in [57]. The wavefunctions are expressed through Jack’s polynomials in the variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_i$$\end{document}xi defined by decomposing the classical variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _n$$\end{document}αn corresponding to the quantum operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_n$$\end{document}an as13.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha _n=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n\alpha }}\left( x_1^n+x_2^n+x_3^n+\cdots \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}αn=1nαx1n+x2n+x3n+⋯,which is reminiscent of the formula (3.5) for the invariant manifolds of the GG Hamiltonian. The eigenstates of the Hamiltonian and other conserved operators are indexed by partitions and the corresponding eigenvalues are expressed by elegant formulas in terms of part lengths [57].

Despite these tantalizing parallels between the structures observed in the GG Hamiltonian and the solution of the quantum Benjamin-Ono Hamiltonian in [57], we have not been able to make immediate use of these parallels. As already mentioned, the construction of conservation laws based on the quantum Lax pair for the GG Hamiltonian runs into divergences when attempting to normal-order the resulting operators. One can furthermore examine the numerically computed integer spectra within specific (N, M)-blocks and try to guess the corresponding formula in terms of part lengths. This does not appear possible, however, even for the very simple blocks with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N=2$$\end{document}N=2 that contain only one nonzero eigenvalue [6]. Intuitively, one should keep in mind the mode \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0$$\end{document}a0 absent in the Benjamin-Ono Hamiltonian but present in the GG Hamiltonian. In the Fock states, the occupation number of this mode \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _0$$\end{document}η0 encodes the number of parts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N-\eta _0$$\end{document}N-η0 in the corresponding partition. Apparently, the eigenvalue formulas must be sensitive to the number of parts, and not only to the part lengths as in [57].

Conclusions

We have undertaken studies of integrability properties of the Hamiltonian (1.1) and its partner Hamiltonian (1.3), relying on a mixture of analytic methods and numerical experimentation, and revealing rich and surprising algebraic structures. While we have not been able to solve the model in the sense of giving an explicit construction of all of its eigenfunctions and the corresponding eigenvalues, a number of prominent patterns have been identified. This includes the decompositions (5.15) and (5.17) providing energy bounds, explicit construction of many families of eigenvectors in Sects. 7, 8 and 10, and alluding to their connections to classical invariant manifolds in Sect. 11, the ladder operator (8.6), the quantum Lax pair (12.1) capable of generating higher conservation law, but requiring subtraction of divergent terms arising in the course of normal ordering, and empirically discovered conservation laws (12.9) and (12.13).

We hope that further studies of this system will supply the missing pieces of the puzzle, and similarities with the quantum Benjamin-Ono equation fully solved in [57] are particularly promising in this regard. One ingredient that has been missing from our construction is the r-matrix theory, which has been systematically used in some cases for generating the Hamiltonian eigenstates, cf. the considerations for quantum bosons with contact interactions in [63, 64]. There is also a distinction between the Lax pairs (3.7) and (12.1) that our considerations revolve around, formulated as operators acting on an infinite-dimensional auxiliary space (this construction goes back to the original presentation due to Gérard and Grellier in [1]), and finite-sized Lax matrices depending on a spectral parameter commonly seen in treatments of inverse scattering theory. It could be useful to find reformulations of our algebraic structures that would bridge this gap with other approaches.

The Gérard-Grellier Hamiltonian forms a very special point in the space of theories (justifying the term ‘superintegrable’ we have been employing to characterize it) and admits deformations preserving its Lax integrability [29, 30, 33]. Once its quantum integrability has been brought fully under control, those deformations will emerge as natural further targets for study.
